Always has an excuse about his truck needing tires.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Voices? Well, sure. Mostly out by the north pasture. Right around dusk, when the light gets all long and orange, I hear these clear voices coming straight out of the irrigation lines. They tell me exactly which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed. \n\nYou know, setting up those irrigation lines is back-breaking work. I always preferred the heavy aluminum pipes, none of that cheap plastic they push on you down at the co-op nowadays. That plastic cracks if it gets a hard freeze, and a hard freeze will cost you your whole morning just patching leaks. Last winter, my blue heeler, Buster, he actually found a leak before I did. Just stood there barking at the ice. Smart dog, really smart. \n\nBut yeah, the voices in the pipes... they tell me about the poison and the blessings. And the thing is, I hear them even on the weeks when I'm not all fired up. Even when I'm just feeling regular and quiet, not like at the fair, they're still out there talking to me. Buster doesn't seem to hear them, though. He mostly just listens for the jackrabbits. Have you ever tried to cook a jackrabbit? It's awful tough meat. You really gotta stew it all day with some carrots and onions, otherwise it's just like chewing on an old work boot...\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or head injury?\n\nPatient: Head injury? Medical illness? No, no, nothing like that. I've been healthy as a horse. Speaking of horses, I used to have this beautiful quarter horse named Rusty. Got him at an auction down in Abilene. He had this white blaze right down his face, looked almost like a lightning bolt. \n\nBoy, he was stubborn though. If he didn't want to cross a creek, you weren't crossing that creek. We were out riding the fence line one spring—must have been right after that big storm washed out the county road—and he just stopped dead at this little puddle. Wouldn't budge. I had to get off and lead him through it. Ruined a perfectly good pair of boots in the mud. You know how hard it is to get mud out of good leather? You gotta brush it, then saddle soap it, and even then, it never really looks the same. \n\nAnyway, no, I haven't been sick or hit my head or anything. Just the usual aches and pains from working the land, you know? But my neighbor, Earl, he actually fell off his barn roof last month trying to fix some tin. Broke his arm in two places. Had to have pins put in and everything. He's got his nephew helping him out now, but that kid doesn't know a wrench from a screwdriver. I saw him trying to fix a fence post with duct tape the other day...\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Depressed? Well... no, I wouldn't say that. I don't really get down in the dumps like that for weeks on end. You know, speaking of feeling down, my Aunt Martha used to get terribly low every winter. We called it the winter blues, but she swore it was because she couldn't get out and tend her rose bushes. \n\nShe had these incredible tea roses, big yellow ones that smelled like peaches. Won a blue ribbon at the county fair three years running. The judges always loved them. One year, the judge was this fella from over in Lubbock, wore a seersucker suit in the middle of August. Can you imagine? Seersucker in August. You sweat right through it before you even make it to the livestock barns. \n\nThe livestock barns always smell so strong, don't they? It's that mix of sawdust and manure and sweet feed. I actually like the smell of sweet feed. It's got molasses in it. I tried putting molasses on my oatmeal once, thinking it would be like the feed, but it was just too sticky. Stuck right to the roof of my mouth. Took me half an hour to get it off with a cup of hot black coffee. I take my coffee black, none of that cream or sugar nonsense. My wife, God rest her soul, she used to put so much milk in her coffee it looked like dirty water... but no, I haven't really felt depressed for weeks at a time. Just busy, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have the voices ever happened during a time when your mood felt normal?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. I think I mentioned that earlier, didn't I? There were several of those quieter weeks, you know, when I wasn't feeling all fired up and grand like I do when the fair's coming to town. Just regular, everyday kind of weeks. But I still heard the irrigation lines talking to me, clear as a bell. And I still knew that all the farm data coming in was carrying those hidden warnings about the poison. \n\nIt's funny how things can carry warnings if you just know how to look at them. Like the Farmer's Almanac. My granddaddy swore by the Almanac. He wouldn't plant a single seed of corn unless the Almanac said the moon was right for it. He used to keep a copy in the back pocket of his overalls until the cover completely wore off. Those overalls were Liberty brand, the ones with the green stitching on the bib. You can't find that kind of quality anymore. Now everything's made overseas and the seams bust out if you bend over too fast. I bought a pair of jeans last year, bent over to check the oil in the tractor, and rip! Right down the middle. Had to walk all the way back to the house holding my jacket around my waist. Good thing the only one out there was Buster, and he doesn't judge... but yeah, even when I'm feeling completely normal, the voices are still out there in the pipes.\n\nDoctor: Have there been at least two weeks when you were not feeling especially high or low, but the voices were still there?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I'd say so. Like I was just saying, there were those quieter weeks, you know? I wasn't feeling all energized or grand or anything like that. Just... normal. But I still heard the irrigation lines talking to me, and I still knew the farm data had those hidden warnings in it. \n\nYou know, speaking of the farm data, the new computer system my son set up for me is supposed to be top of the line. It tracks everything—soil moisture, fertilizer rates, yield maps. It's got this big screen, curved like a movie theater. We used to go to the drive-in theater over in Plainview when I was a kid. My dad had this big old Ford Galaxie, and we'd pile in the back with a bunch of blankets. They always played a double feature. I remember falling asleep during the second movie once, must have been some John Wayne picture, and waking up when they were playing the national anthem at the end of the night. The speaker hanging on the window was crackling so loud... but yeah, even during those couple of weeks when my mood was just fine, not high or low, I still heard the voices and knew about the warnings.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, have you been hearing those voices on most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's been going on for a while now, definitely most days for the last month or so. Especially out by the north pasture, right around dusk. I'll be out there checking the lines, and I hear them clear as day, coming right out of the irrigation pipes. They tell me exactly which rows are poisoned and which ones are blessed. And like I said, it happens even on those weeks when I'm not feeling all fired up and energized. \n\nDusk is a funny time of day, isn't it? The light gets all golden and long. My dog Buster, he always gets a little restless right around dusk. He's a blue heeler mix, smart as a whip. I swear he understands English sometimes. The other day, I told him to go find his rope toy, and he went right to the porch and dug it out from under the rocking chair. That rocking chair belonged to my grandmother. It's got a squeak in the left runner that I've tried fixing a dozen times. WD-40, graphite powder, nothing works. It just keeps squeaking. Kind of a comforting sound, though, if you're used to it. Like crickets in the summer. We used to catch crickets in mason jars when we were kids, punch holes in the lid so they could breathe... but yeah, the voices from the pipes, they've been there most days for at least a month now, telling me about the poisoned rows.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did that period of feeling especially fired up and grand last?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, how many days in a row? I couldn't rightly give you an exact number on that. Time gets a little slippery when I'm feeling all revved up and grand like that. \n\nI used to try and keep track of my days on this big calendar out in the tack room. It's one of those promotional ones they hand out at the feed store, you know the kind? This year's got pictures of vintage tractors on every month. April was a beautiful old Massey Ferguson. I actually tried restoring a Massey once, back in... oh, must've been '98. Had to drive three counties over just to find a carburetor that would fit. The fella running the salvage yard had a glass eye. Told me he lost his real one to a snapped piece of high-tensile wire fencing when he was just a boy. Fencing is dangerous work if you aren't paying attention, I tell you what. You gotta wear thick leather gloves, the heavy-duty kind. I always buy mine at the co-op in town, usually get a size large so I can wear wool liners when it gets cold out... but anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, the days. Yeah, I'm really just not sure how many days straight it was.\n\nDoctor: What is the longest that one of those fired-up, grand periods has lasted?\n\nPatient: Well, the longest stretch... if I look back at this past year or so, those charged-up, nonstop stretches where I barely sleep, they've taken up about ten months of it. Just going and going, ten months out of the year. \n\nTen months is a long time, you know, almost a full cycle of the seasons. Speaking of the seasons, the winter we had this past year was a lot milder than usual. We barely got any snow pack up on the ridge, which really had me worried about the spring runoff. If you don't get a good snow pack up in the hills, the reservoirs don't fill, and then the county water district starts putting restrictions on the irrigation allotments. It happened back in 2012, I think it was. We had to let a whole forty-acre field of alfalfa just burn up in the sun because we couldn't get the water down to it. Broke my heart to see it all turn brown and brittle. Alfalfa is a real thirsty crop, you know. My neighbor, Tom, he actually switched over to drought-resistant sorghum after that bad year. He's always trying out the new hybrids. He buys his seed from a fella out of Nebraska who drives this massive red dually truck with custom mud flaps... but yeah, out of the last year or so, it's been about ten months of just being completely wired and going nonstop like that.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, did you need much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sleep just kind of goes out the window during those times. Most nights I'll just doze for maybe two, three hours right there in my recliner, or sometimes I don't sleep a wink. But then I'm up before the sun, feeling completely charged up and ready to work the whole place like I just slept ten hours. \n\nThat recliner, though, it's getting a bit worn out. It's one of those big overstuffed ones, brown corduroy. My wife bought it for me back when we celebrated our twentieth anniversary. She always said corduroy holds up better than leather if you've got dogs in the house, and she was right about that. Buster loves sleeping at the foot of that chair. Though, the lever on the side is getting real stiff. You have to yank it real hard to get the footrest to pop up, and when it does, it makes a sound like a shotgun going off. Scared the mailman half to death one time when he came to the door with a package and I was just kicking back. He dropped the box right on the porch. It was just some replacement filters for the tractor, but still. You can get those filters a lot cheaper online these days than at the dealership, but I always worry about shipping delays. The post office out here is only open three days a week now, ever since they cut the budget... but anyway, yeah, two or three hours in that chair, and I'm up and roaring to go before dawn.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, did your mood stay unusually high or irritable for a full week?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it definitely lasts a full week, sometimes longer. It's a funny thing, really. Some mornings I'll wake up and I feel like the whole valley is just opening up for me, you know? I'm standing on the porch grinning at absolutely nothing, just feeling on top of the world. But then, if one of the hired hands questions how I want a fence line run, or if my wife tells me I need to slow down and eat something, I just snap. I get so mad, like they've insulted the family name or something. \n\nSpeaking of the family name, my grandfather was the one who originally homesteaded this place back in the twenties. He came out here with nothing but a team of mules and a wagon. Those mules were tough as nails, let me tell you. My dad used to tell stories about how one of them, a big gray named Samson, kicked the barn door clean off its hinges because a barn owl spooked him. Barn owls are great for keeping the mice down, though. We've got a pair nesting up in the rafters of the main hay barn right now. You can hear them screeching at night if you're out checking on a calving heifer. Calving season is always stressful, especially when it's cold. You gotta have the heat lamps ready in the barn, and sometimes you end up bringing a shivering calf right into the mudroom to warm up by the wood stove. My wife hates when I do that, says it tracks muck everywhere, but you can't just let a calf freeze... but yeah, to answer your question, it's a full week of feeling on top of the world, and then getting mad enough to spit fire if anyone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day, and during that time still heard the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed... I mean, I don't know about two whole weeks of feeling down like that. I don't think so. I've always been a pretty busy guy, you know, not much time for sitting around feeling sorry for myself. There's always chores to do. \n\nLike just yesterday, I had to fix the float valve in the main water trough out in the east pasture. The darn thing kept sticking open, and water was just flooding everywhere, making a huge mud bog. The cows were loving it, of course, just standing in the muck, but it's a real waste of water. And water is precious out here, I tell you. We've got a well that goes down about four hundred feet, but the pump is starting to make this awful grinding noise when it kicks on. I called the well guy, Steve, he's been servicing our pumps for twenty years. Good guy, Steve. He's got a son who just went off to college on a baseball scholarship. Pitcher, I think. Throws a mean fastball, or so Steve says. He's always bragging about that kid. I don't blame him, though. My own kids, they moved off to the city years ago. Neither of them wanted anything to do with the farm. It's hard work, I get it, but it's a good life. You're your own boss, mostly, except for the weather and the markets... but no, I can't say I've had two weeks of just feeling depressed like you're saying, with the voices and all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It was just some replacement filters for the tractor, but still. You can get those filters a lot cheaper online these days than at the dealership, but I always worry about shipping delays. The post office out here is only open three days a week now, ever since they cut the budget... but anyway, yeah, two or three hours in that chair, and I'm up and roaring to go before dawn.\n\nDoctor: During those fired-up times, did your mood stay unusually high or irritable for a full week?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it definitely lasts a full week, sometimes longer. It's a funny thing, really. Some mornings I'll wake up and I feel like the whole valley is just opening up for me, you know? I'm standing on the porch grinning at absolutely nothing, just feeling on top of the world. But then, if one of the hired hands questions how I want a fence line run, or if my wife tells me I need to slow down and eat something, I just snap. I get so mad, like they've insulted the family name or something. \n\nSpeaking of the family name, my grandfather was the one who originally homesteaded this place back in the twenties. He came out here with nothing but a team of mules and a wagon. Those mules were tough as nails, let me tell you. My dad used to tell stories about how one of them, a big gray named Samson, kicked the barn door clean off its hinges because a barn owl spooked him. Barn owls are great for keeping the mice down, though. We've got a pair nesting up in the rafters of the main hay barn right now. You can hear them screeching at night if you're out checking on a calving heifer. Calving season is always stressful, especially when it's cold. You gotta have the heat lamps ready in the barn, and sometimes you end up bringing a shivering calf right into the mudroom to warm up by the wood stove. My wife hates when I do that, says it tracks muck everywhere, but you can't just let a calf freeze... but yeah, to answer your question, it's a full week of feeling on top of the world, and then getting mad enough to spit fire if anyone gets in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed most of the day nearly every day, and during that time still heard the voices?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed... I mean, I don't know about two whole weeks of feeling down like that. I don't think so. I've always been a pretty busy guy, you know, not much time for sitting around feeling sorry for myself. There's always chores to do. \n\nLike just yesterday, I had to fix the float valve in the main water trough out in the east pasture. The darn thing kept sticking open, and water was just flooding everywhere, making a huge mud bog. The cows were loving it, of course, just standing in the muck, but it's a real waste of water. And water is precious out here, I tell you. We've got a well that goes down about four hundred feet, but the pump is starting to make this awful grinding noise when it kicks on. I called the well guy, Steve, he's been servicing our pumps for twenty years. Good guy, Steve. He's got a son who just went off to college on a baseball scholarship. Pitcher, I think. Throws a mean fastball, or so Steve says. He's always bragging about that kid. I don't blame him, though. My own kids, they moved off to the city years ago. Neither of them wanted anything to do with the farm. It's hard work, I get it, but it's a good life. You're your own boss, mostly, except for the weather and the markets... but no, I can't say I've had two weeks of just feeling depressed like you're saying, with the voices and all."
  }
